### Comment by [@dkaluza](https://github.com/dkaluza) — 2026-08-18T16:29:01Z

I took a quick look at this KEP
I would personally say this is orthogonal to https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/kueue/issues/13396 (Configurable Preemptions)  - the ability to partially preempt the elastic workloads is an added value here and we did not plan it in any way in the initial Configurable Preemptions proposal.

In the targeted solution I would envision this as a flag in the `PreemptionConfig`s that indicates if whole workloads should be preempted or they should be reduced to minCount if possible before full preemption. 

But the scope of Configurable Preemptions is already huge and growing as we delve into the details, so I would rather leave it as a future extension when we have the first version of the framework already available.

It is hard to judge if this is worth it to add to existing preemptions in the meantime until we have the configurable preemptions "mature" enough to cover the existing preemption rules. From my point of view it should be judged by the maintainers - where they wish to allocate the reviewing capacity & how they envision near future of the project.

### Comment by [@zhengchenyu](https://github.com/zhengchenyu) — 2026-10-20T02:50:05Z

@sweinberg-netflix, glad to see partial preemption made it into v0.21.

I'm fine with you taking the lead. We've already completed the Spark DRA adaptation and make many bug fixes and optimizations, so I'd like to keep building on the current approach. If you head based on the current approach, it is better for other job frameworks other than spark. I'll rebase our latest internal version inot #14590. Does that work for you? 
I'd really like to be deeply involved here; we can provider Spark DRA experience at large scale in production environment.

Let me first introduce my current implementation to make the discussion easier:

(1) How partial preemption is triggered
- The partial preemption trigger is exactly the same as a normal full eviction.
- When a candidate workload configured with annotation and its current count > minCount, it will partial preemption.
- Partial-preemptible jobs are prioritized as preemption candidates in the ordering.

(2) How partial preemption is executed
- Handling the reduced workload in the snapshot: remove the original full workload (or the previous round's reduced workload), add the reduced workload scaled down to the target count.
- Configurable preemption granularity:
  - On-demand mode: a binary search for the smallest scale-down that lets the preemptor just fit.
  - Step mode: we can scale down to minCount directly or scale down by step via maxReclaimedCount
- Timeout fallback to full eviction: if a partial preemption htimeout, fall back to whole-workload eviction.
- Multi-round candidate selection: since a job may first be partially preempted and later fully evicted, candidate selection was changed from a single round to multiple iterative rounds.
- Emitting reclaimTargetCount: written back to the workload as a scale-down hint for the job.
- Release phase: Kueue detects when the actual count <= the reclaimTargetCount, then clears reclaimTargetCount; while reclaimTargetCount exists, scale-up will be rejected.

(3) How partial preemption is responded to
- (a) The spark driver reads reclaimTargetCount from the workload directly and scales itself down.
  - Note: this is the approach we use internally. Because we don't want to produce extra pending pods, our Spark driver is workload-aware.

For (1) and (2), I personally think they're good, but please review the approach as well. For (3), I think our internal workload-aware approach isn't suitable for the community . So I think there's still the following work to do:

- Refining the "How partial preemption is responded to" approach:
  - (b) Following what @reruno proposed in #1727: Kueue deletes Pods directly based on reclaimTargetCount; if the job runtime produces extra Pods again, they're held via a scheduling gate.
  - (c) Set the job's spec.Count directly.
  I personally like (b), and it's also more favorable for integrating with TAS later; I think (c) violates the design principle, but it is easy for testing.
- Introducing a preemption.minCount / floor independent of minCount, to distinguish the preemption floor from the partial-admission floor.
- Resort the candidates after partial preemption; I haven't implemented this yet, because of the significant performance impact.
- Supporting multiple podsets.
- Adapting more job runtimes.

### Comment by [@Pradeep39](https://github.com/Pradeep39) — 2026-10-22T04:53:00Z

> Refining the "How partial preemption is responded to" approach:
> * (b) Following what @reruno proposed in [Doc: Add PodsReadyRequeueStrategy documentation #1727](https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/kueue/pull/1727): Kueue deletes Pods directly based on reclaimTargetCount; if the job runtime produces extra Pods again, they're held via a scheduling gate.

@zhengchenyu - Thank you for considering backward compatibility with Spark Maintenance versions (eg: 3.4.3) and refining how partial preemption is responded to. 

One improvement I would suggest is to use the EvictionsAPI as opposed to issuing a plain delete of the Pod from the Kueue framework integration. EvictionsAPI is designed for situations where a custom controller wishes to safely evict pods without directly talking to the parent controller. It goes through a PodDisruptionBudget (PDB) evaluation, which in Spark case is not set. So the API server will always accept the eviction request and marks the pod with a deletionTimestamp and a grace period. The kubelet on the respective node then gracefully shuts down the pod.  The parent controller (Spark Driver) detects that one of its managed pods has been marked for deletion. To maintain its declared state, the controller automatically spins up a brand-new replacement pod which will remain gated. Additionally during decomissioning, Spark will try to migrate all the RDD blocks (controlled by spark.storage.decommission.rddBlocks.enabled) and shuffle blocks (controlled by spark.storage.decommission.shuffleBlocks.enabled) from the decommissioning executor to a remote executor when spark.storage.decommission.enabled is enabled. Perhaps forcing these properties to true will make partial Preemption super safe operation.

I have a working reference implementation of all of the above in my [repo](https://github.com/Pradeep39/kueue/tree/spark-aware-preemption). Here is a demo.

<img width="1536" height="764" alt="Demo of the partial preemption (compressed, 1536px)" src="https://github.com/user-attachments/assets/79acb5a6-d89e-4cc9-a6b9-31849e15a9f9" />

### Comment by [@reruno](https://github.com/reruno) — 2026-10-22T09:34:42Z

Partial preemption could be split into 2 small sub tasks. Picking victims and scaling down. Let's put picking victims aside and focus on scaling down problem for now. 

If I understand correctly scaling down with your approach @zhengchenyu needs some collaboration from spark driver (not kueue owned code) (correct me if I am wrong). 

I have created draft POC with help of AI that does not require collaboration from spark driver, here are the stages of how scaling down works in my draft POC (https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/kueue/pull/17484 working partial preemption for Ray and Spark)
```
Kueue forcefully removes executor pods + lowers the workload's pod count (this reclaims quota) ->
job runtime (spark driver) creates new pod ->
kueue automatically gate it (here the state of workload is similar to pre state for partial scale up feature) ->
partial scale up feature scales up the workload when quota is available
``` 

I suggest maybe for alpha version we could use my approach for scaling down, and later in the future we could add code to spark driver upstream for improved performance. 

https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/kueue/pull/17484 I will be doing some cleaning up for this PR so please don't review code, I really want to hear your opinion for the approach of scaling down. Pros and cons of my approach of scaling down from what I am seeing
Pros:  
- Plugs into partial scale up cleanly (after partial preemption, partial scale up picks up the job and when quota is available again partial scale up scales up the workload) , because my approach gates the pods, and partial scale up feature can ungate them
- Doesn't require collaboration from third party code 

Cons: 
- No graceful decommission (lost in-flight tasks / shuffle data), performance. (but I think later we can add code to spark driver to support high performance mode)

@zhengchenyu  @sweinberg-netflix I would like to hear your opinion on that approach

cc @mimowo @sweinberg-netflix @vladikkuzn @Pradeep39 @mbobrovskyi

>

> 
> * Plugs into partial scale up cleanly (after partial preemption, partial scale up picks up the job and when quota is available again partial scale up scales up the workload) , because my approach gates the pods, and partial scale up feature can ungate them
> * Doesn't require collaboration from third party code
> 
> Cons:
> 
> * No graceful decommission (lost in-flight tasks / shuffle data), performance. (but I think later we can add code to spark driver to support high performance mode)
>

@reruno +1 on this approach. Its biggest advantage is that it needs no changes to Spark or to the Spark operators. A driver-orchestrated approach would mean making the driver Kueue-aware, through a driver plugin or changes upstream in Spark, which is a much bigger lift. That can still come later as an optimization.

I also wouldn't list graceful decommissioning as a con: it works with your approach today. Since Spark 3.1, spark.decommission.enabled=true makes Spark add a preStop hook to each executor Pod. When Kueue deletes or evicts the Pod, the kubelet runs that hook, the executor stops taking new tasks, and with spark.storage.decommission.shuffleBlocks.enabled and spark.storage.decommission.rddBlocks.enabled it migrates its shuffle and cached blocks to the surviving executors, all within the Pod's termination grace period. My reference implementation has Kueue's webhook set these properties on reclaimable applications when they're created, leaving any value the user sets: https://github.com/Pradeep39/kueue/blob/5f68f86a8fb338e2bb788a89b6a49bbb18780450/pkg/controller/jobs/sparkapplication/sparkapplication_partialpreemption.go#L56-L64. In our runs the reclaimed executors handed over their blocks with 0 fetch failures and 0 resubmitted stages. This works with a plain Delete or the Eviction API. I'd recommend the Eviction API, for the reasons in my earlier comment.

One suggestion on the ordering: lower the Workload's count only after the reclaimed Pods have actually stopped, rather than when they're deleted. With graceful decommissioning the Pods keep running for up to their grace period, so releasing quota at deletion lets the preemptor be admitted onto capacity they still hold. My implementation releases it once the Pods are gone, with a timeout as a backstop if they don't stop. 

Repo: https://github.com/Pradeep39/kueue/tree/spark-aware-preemption
Reference Implementation (draft PR): [Pradeep39#55](https://github.com/Pradeep39/kueue/pull/55)

### Comment by [@Pradeep39](https://github.com/Pradeep39) — 2026-10-22T16:54:17Z

> Suggesting a redesign around reusing existing mechanisms; details inline. Summary:
> 
> 1. Reuse the elastic scale-down path of `ElasticJobsViaWorkloadSlices`.
> 2. Keep one target mode: the largest target that fits.
> 3. Drop the max-reclaimed-count annotation.
> 4. Replace `minCount` with a dedicated preemption floor.
> 5. Don't add a new candidate-ordering tier.
> 6. Keep the timeout, but make it a real API with a safe default.
> 
> * Blocking: Timeout fallback can't fire.
> * Blocking: Missing sections.
> * Blocking: kep.yaml gaps.
> 
> _This review was prepared with AI assistance (Claude Code)._

+1 on reclaiming only what's needed and dropping the cut-to-floor default. My concern is how "largest target that fits" applies across victims. Applied candidate by candidate, it takes as much as it can from the first candidate before touching the next, so the loss lands on whichever victim comes first in the ordering.

I'd propose spreading the loss instead. Leave candidate selection exactly as it is, classic or configurable (KEP-13396), with no new ordering tier, in line with # 5. Then, among the selected candidates, take one Pod per candidate per round, never going below a candidate's floor, until the preemptor fits. The total reclaimed is the same, still only what's needed; it's just shared across the victims rather than concentrated on one. For Spark that's the difference between several apps each losing an executor and one app losing most of its executors. In my reference implementation demo included in my earlier comment, ten victims each gave one executor, and the two largest a second. The default classic preemption on the same workload evicts three whole applications.

### Comment by [@zhengchenyu](https://github.com/zhengchenyu) — 2026-10-23T02:18:37Z

@reruno @Pradeep39 
Partial preemption could be split into 2 small sub tasks. Picking victims and scaling down. Let me focus on scaling down! 
In fact, I previously mentioned two approaches in my comments:
* Proactive downscaling
* Reactive downscaling

My KEP primarily covered proactive scaling; I think we should also add reactive scaling into the KEP. Additionally, feel free to update this KEP—you can submit a "suggested commit" directly.

### Proactive downscaling

In the proactive scaling model, the job runtime releases quota by explicitly reducing `spec.Count`.
The Spark driver scenario I described earlier follows this approach. Additionally, in #14590, I implemented proactive scaling for Kubernetes batch job with `RespondToPartialPreemption`.

### Reactive downscaling
This approach was proposed by @reruno and serves as a general-purpose solution.
And I agree @Pradeep39's proposal to use PDBs and the Evictions API, it's more graceful.

### Pros and Cons:
| | pros | cons|
|---|---|---|
|Proactive downscaling|The job runtime can proactively select which pods to delete, more graceful.|It requires development support from the job runtime itself.|
|Reactive downscaling| This is a general-purpose solution; there is no need to develop within the job runtime.| less graceful, as the Kueue side cannot discern the job, which may lead to the loss of computation.|

Could we move forward with the core functionality by starting with the proactive scaling for Kubernetes batch job as implemented in #14590? Reactive downscaling still requires lots of work; it should be addressed after #14590. 

And I will update the dep, add `Reactive downscaling`.

Then I also think we need independent `floor` value instead of `minCount`, I will change the KEP.  cc: @Pradeep39 @vladikkuzn

Aditional, feel free to commit suggestion on  #14590, or rebase on #14590, I have test in real cluster with the cases: https://github.com/zhengchenyu/cases/tree/main/kueue/partial-preempt. I hope this feature can be merged into the main, as we would like to update our version.

* OnDemandPreemption

### Comment by [@mimowo](https://github.com/mimowo) — 2026-10-23T20:03:53Z

Re 

> Proactive downscaling	The job runtime can proactively select which pods to delete, more graceful.	It requires development support from the job runtime itself.
> Reactive downscaling	This is a general-purpose solution; there is no need to develop within the job runtime.	less graceful, as the Kueue side cannot discern the job, which may lead to the loss of computation.

This is nice summary. I'm wondering that this "Proactive downscaling" seems like substantially more work, because this is integration specific. We may also run into conflict with the autoscaler - I'm thinking Ray autoscaler, so Kueue evicts and then immediately after Ray autoscaler inscreases the size based on load causing trashing.

Also, containers still could dump their data using  `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` after SIGTERM sent by the Eviction API in cases of "Reactive downscaling".

### Comment by [@Pradeep39](https://github.com/Pradeep39) — 2026-10-24T02:14:35Z

> Re
> 
> > Proactive downscaling	The job runtime can proactively select which pods to delete, more graceful.	It requires development support from the job runtime itself.
> > Reactive downscaling	This is a general-purpose solution; there is no need to develop within the job runtime.	less graceful, as the Kueue side cannot discern the job, which may lead to the loss of computation.
> 
> This is nice summary. I'm wondering that this "Proactive downscaling" seems like substantially more work, because this is integration specific. We may also run into conflict with the autoscaler - I'm thinking Ray autoscaler, so Kueue evicts and then immediately after Ray autoscaler inscreases the size based on load causing trashing.
> 
> Also, containers still could dump their data using `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` after SIGTERM sent by the Eviction API in cases of "Reactive downscaling".

@mimowo / @zhengchenyu, for Spark I don't see much difference between the reactive and proactive approaches, because Spark already turns an eviction or delete into a graceful decommission. With spark.decommission.enabled=true and the related storage-decommission properties (Spark 3.1+), which a webhook can inject, every executor Pod gets a preStop hook. The hook runs /opt/decom.sh, which signals the executor to report its imminent exit to the driver and migrate its shuffle data before terminationGracePeriodSeconds runs out and SIGKILL is sent. So an Eviction or Delete API call already makes the executor decommission itself graceful.

That's the same decommission path a driver-initiated scale-down would use, and in both approaches the trigger is Kueue's reclaimTargetCount. The main reason that would remain to pursue proactive downscaling would be control over which executors go. Perhaps, that could be modeled on spark.kubernetes.executor.rollPolicy, which [ExecutorRollPlugin](https://github.com/apache/spark/blob/12f3c81c26ef639842b8a155e5fd5ccfa7705bea/resource-managers/kubernetes/core/src/main/scala/org/apache/spark/scheduler/cluster/k8s/ExecutorRollPlugin.scala#L84) uses to pick the executor to decommission (e.g. by ID, add time, GC time, or as an outlier) when it periodically rolls executors in long-running applications on Kubernetes.

So I'd keep reactive downscaling through the Eviction API as the general mechanism for Spark, and leave proactive downscaling to runtimes that need finer pod selection. If the remaining concern is that a Kueue integration controller is evicting Pods owned by another controller, that is what the Eviction API is for:  the cluster autoscaler, the descheduler and other custom controllers all use it to remove Pods they don't own, while respecting `PodDisruptionBudgets`. To address it further, the SparkApplication integration could be moved out of the Kueue tree, to its own repository alongside the Spark operator, deployed as an external framework (integrations.externalFrameworks).

On autoscaler thrashing: I'm not sure about Ray, but in Spark's case Spark's Dynamic Allocation ( Ref: #17127 ) will also request replacements. In the elastic integration they stay gated beyond the grant, and tuning spark.kubernetes.allocation.maxPendingPods which defaults to int.MaxValue caps how many the driver keeps pending. The gated Pods still count towards the requested size, though, so a scale-up can still preempt again. Neither proactive nor reactive approach solves that part.

cc @reruno

### Comment by [@zhengchenyu](https://github.com/zhengchenyu) — 2026-10-26T01:58:20Z

> Re
> 
> > Proactive downscaling	The job runtime can proactively select which pods to delete, more graceful.	It requires development support from the job runtime itself.
> > Reactive downscaling	This is a general-purpose solution; there is no need to develop within the job runtime.	less graceful, as the Kueue side cannot discern the job, which may lead to the loss of computation.
> 
> This is nice summary. I'm wondering that this "Proactive downscaling" seems like substantially more work, because this is integration specific. We may also run into conflict with the autoscaler - I'm thinking Ray autoscaler, so Kueue evicts and then immediately after Ray autoscaler inscreases the size based on load causing trashing.
> 
> Also, containers still could dump their data using `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` after SIGTERM sent by the Eviction API in cases of "Reactive downscaling".
@mimowo 
In my view:
* (1) Proactive downscaling should be handled entirely by the job framework; this applies to scenarios where the job itself is aware of its own workload. 
For instance, if a Spark driver or Ray autoscaler needs to be aware of `spec.Count`, `admit.Count`, or `reclaimTargetCount`, some integrated development should be required. This approach is primarily used for precise control, helping to avoid the creation of excessive Custom Resources (such as pending pods). 
Note: If Ray wants to precise control based on workload, it should integrate Kueue’s functionality into the Ray autoscaler, shouldn’t be any conflict.

* (2) Reactive downscaling should be managed by Kueue; this should be considered Kueue’s default behavior. While conflicts can certainly arise, as @reruno described, since the downscaling has already occurred, any new pods generated by the Ray autoscaler will enter a `SchedulingGate` state and will not consume additional quota.
Note: I know https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/kueue/pull/17127 which is just the Reactive downscaling. I agree with this!

> (1) is the solution we implemented internally for Spark. (2) is the approach proposed by @reruno. I think (2) represents the primary default behavior of the Kueue community, while (1) refers to optional behaviors specific to frameworks.


@Pradeep39 
I have always maintained that Spark should default to reactive downscaling in #17127, and that Kueue’s approach should primarily rely on reactive downscaling.
Why do I propose both active and passive scaling-down?
As we calculate quotas using `min(spec.Count, admit.Count)`, we can release quota by reducing either `spec.Count` or `admit.Count`.
Proactive downscaling: If the `JobRuntime` is workload-aware, it can read the `reclaimTargetCount` and reduce `spec.Count` to release quota.
Reactive downscaling: If Kueue itself reduces `admit.Count`—effectively killing pods—any newly arriving pods will be set to `schedulingGated`.
Since proactive downscaling is already supported by the `main` branch, I have implemented this approach for Kubernetes Jobs in my PR.

### Comment by [@sweinberg-netflix](https://github.com/sweinberg-netflix) — 2026-10-26T17:55:20Z

(Excuse if I am repeating what has already been said)
@reruno I really like the design you put together for Ray for "reactive downscaling" #17484  since Ray doesn't give an outside controller a clean way to scale a cluster down while the autoscaler is running.

For the sake of argument I looked into options for what "proactive(ish) dowscaling", for  Ray, but none of them are particularly clean:
- Kueue lowers `workerGroupSpecs[].replicas` to `reclaimTargetCount`. This doesn't work with autoscaling: KubeRay doesn't delete Pods when `replicas` drops while the autoscaler is on, and the autoscaler just resets it.
- Kueue temporarily lowers `workerGroupSpecs[].maxReplicas`. This also means Kueue edits the cluster spec directly, which I'd like to avoid because it muddies what the user set as the desired state. In theory, Kueue could restore `maxReplicas` once the scale-down finishes and keep any new Pods gated. But this feels quite brittle to me: if Kueue crashes or loses track before restoring it, the RayCluster spec is left in the wrong state.
- Kueue writes `workerGroupSpecs[].scaleStrategy.workersToDelete`. The Ray autoscaler owns this field, so the two could race. For example, the v2 autoscaler refuses new terminations while deletions are pending in that worker group. It also needs explicit Pod names, so Kueue still picks which Pods go, which makes it a worse version of what you're proposing.
- Ray reads `reclaimTargetCount` directly and scales down on its own. This is the proactive approach, and I see its merits, but it would require significant upstream changes.

Ultimately, in my opinion, what you have fits most cleanly with partial preemption and elastic workloads, so it works for me.

cc @zhengchenyu @mimowo

### Comment by [@zhengchenyu](https://github.com/zhengchenyu) — 2026-10-27T01:55:33Z

Since there is a consensus that "reactive downscaling" is the ultimate solution, I propose the following development roadmap:
The current code in the `main` branch lacks logic for reducing `admit.Count`, but it does share logic with elastic jobs for reducing `spec.Count`. Therefore, I suggest the following steps:
* Step 1: Implement the core functionality for selecting "victims" (workloads to be scaled down) to determine the `reclaimTargetCount`. This can be demonstrated using "proactive downscaling"—similar to the implementation in https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/kueue/pull/14590 which establishes the basic mechanism and allows for validation using Kubernetes Batch Jobs.
> Note: We do not need to account for the integration of third-party job frameworks; those who need "proactive downscaling" can develop it themselves (for example me). It dosen't matter with kueue development.
* Step 2: Implement "reactive downscaling." This is a complex feature that maybe requires:
    * establishing an index mapping workloads to pods. 
    * must carefully evaluate the impact of directly reducing the `admit.Count` value on the existing codebase
    * determine which pod to be evicted. 
    * and so on.
I think @reruno  and @Pradeep39  likely have significant experience on this.
* Step 3: Compatible with more jobs.

How about this steps? @mimowo @sweinberg-netflix @reruno @Pradeep39

@@ -0,0 +1,306 @@
+# KEP-975: Partial Preemption of Elastic Workloads
+
+<!-- toc -->
+- [Summary](#summary)
+- [Motivation](#motivation)
+  - [Goals](#goals)
+  - [Non-Goals](#non-goals)
+- [Proposal](#proposal)
+  - [User Stories](#user-stories)
+    - [Story 1: Spark on Kubernetes elastic executors](#story-1-spark-on-kubernetes-elastic-executors)
+  - [Notes/Constraints/Caveats](#notesconstraintscaveats)
+  - [Risks and Mitigations](#risks-and-mitigations)
+- [Design Details](#design-details)
+  - [Two-phase model: request != release](#two-phase-model-request--release)
+  - [Workload API](#workload-api)
+  - [Opt-in annotation](#opt-in-annotation)
+  - [Reusing minCount](#reusing-mincount)
+  - [Scheduler / Preemption](#scheduler--preemption)
+    - [Eligibility](#eligibility)
+    - [Candidate ordering](#candidate-ordering)
+    - [Target selection](#target-selection)
+    - [Issuing preemptions](#issuing-preemptions)
+    - [No-fit progress across scheduling cycles](#no-fit-progress-across-scheduling-cycles)
+  - [Webhook](#webhook)
+  - [Job runtime contract](#job-runtime-contract)
+  - [Test Plan](#test-plan)
+    - [Unit tests](#unit-tests)
+    - [Integration tests](#integration-tests)
+    - [e2e tests](#e2e-tests)
+  - [Graduation Criteria](#graduation-criteria)
+- [Implementation History](#implementation-history)
+- [Drawbacks](#drawbacks)
+- [Alternatives](#alternatives)
+  - [Kueue mutates the job's replica count directly](#kueue-mutates-the-jobs-replica-count-directly)
+  - [Incremental reclamation instead of scaling to minCount](#incremental-reclamation-instead-of-scaling-to-mincount)
+<!-- /toc -->
+
+## Summary
+
+Today Kueue preempts a Workload as a whole: the victim is evicted, `stopJob` suspends it, and all
+of its Pods are torn down. For elastic workloads that can run at a reduced size (for example a Spark
+application whose executor count can shrink), whole eviction is unnecessarily disruptive.
+
+**Partial Preemption** lets Kueue reclaim quota from an elastic, opted-in Workload by asking it to
+scale one PodSet **down to its `minCount`** — freeing just enough quota for the preemptor — instead
+of evicting it. The victim keeps running at a smaller size and is never suspended. The feature is
+alpha and gated by the `PartialPreemption` feature gate; when the gate is off, behavior is identical
+to today.
+
+## Motivation
+
+Elastic batch/data workloads (Spark, Ray, and similar) can often continue making progress
+with fewer replicas. When a higher-priority Workload needs quota, Kueue's only tool is to evict a
+victim entirely. For a long-running elastic job this means killing the driver and every worker,
+losing in-flight progress, and paying a full restart — even though releasing a few replicas would
+have been enough.
+
+Kueue cannot shrink a running job on its own: only the job's controller/driver owns its Pods.
+Partial preemption therefore needs a way for Kueue to **signal** a desired reduced size and for the
+job runtime to **act** on it, while the quota accounting stays correct throughout.
+
+### Goals
+
+- Provide an opt-in mechanism where preemption of an elastic Workload reclaims quota by scaling one
+  of its PodSets down to `minCount`, rather than evicting the whole Workload.
+- Guarantee no quota over-subscription at any point during the scale-down.
+- Keep the mechanism framework-agnostic in Kueue: Kueue only expresses intent; any controller/driver
+  that honors the signal can benefit.
+- Be fully gated so that, with the gate disabled, there is zero behavioral change.
+
+### Non-Goals
+
+- Kueue does not itself terminate Pods or mutate the parent job's spec; the job runtime is
+  responsible for shedding replicas (see [Alternatives](#alternatives) for why).
+- Kueue does not guarantee the job respects the requested reduced size; a cooperating runtime is
+  assumed (opt-in).
+- Autoscaling / scaling back up after preemption is out of scope; this KEP only covers scale-down
+  for quota reclamation.
+
+## Proposal
+
+Introduce a per-PodSet target, `status.admission.podSetAssignments[].reclaimTargetCount`, that Kueue
+writes on an admitted elastic Workload to request that the PodSet scale down to that count
+(= `minCount`). The job runtime observes `reclaimTargetCount`, sheds the excess replicas, and as
+those Pods actually terminate the accounted `count` decreases and quota is released — at which point
+the preemptor is admitted.
+
+The preemption decision path (both the classical and the Fair Sharing algorithms) is extended so
+that an eligible elastic victim is simulated as *reduced to `minCount`* in the scheduling snapshot
+instead of *removed*, and such victims are preferred over whole eviction of equal-priority peers.
+
+### User Stories
+
+#### Story 1: Spark on Kubernetes elastic executors
+
+A Spark application runs with 20 executors (`minExecutors=2`) in a low-priority ClusterQueue that is
+borrowing quota. A high-priority job arrives and needs part of that quota. Instead of suspending the
+whole Spark application (killing the driver and all executors and losing progress), Kueue writes
+`reclaimTargetCount=2` on the executor PodSet. The Spark driver gracefully decommissions executors
+down to 2, quota is released as those executor Pods exit, and the high-priority job is admitted. The
+Spark application keeps running the entire time.
+
+### Notes/Constraints/Caveats
+
+- The victim must opt in (annotation) and have a PodSet with `minCount` set, and must currently be
+  using more than `minCount`. Otherwise it is treated as a normal (whole-eviction) preemption target.
+- `reclaimTargetCount` is owned and re-derived by Kueue on every scheduling decision; it is not a
+  user-set field and never holds a stale value across re-admission.
+- Partial preemption interoperates with elastic-job scale-down (the accounted `count` only decreases
+  as Pods actually terminate), which is what keeps accounting correct — see below.
+- **Reclaim granularity (open question).** This proposal scales the reclaimable PodSet all the way
+  down to `minCount` in a single request, even when reclaiming fewer replicas would already free
+  enough quota for the preemptor. A progressive/incremental variant — reclaiming only as many
+  replicas as needed, with `minCount` as the floor — could reduce disruption but complicates the
+  accumulate-and-fit search and the convergence argument. This is left as an open question for the
+  alpha review.
+
+### Risks and Mitigations
+
+- **Over-subscription window.** Naively releasing quota when the request is written would let the
+  preemptor and the not-yet-shrunk victim overlap. Mitigated by the [two-phase
+  model](#two-phase-model-request--release): the request is decoupled from the release; accounted
+  `count` only drops as Pods exit.
+- **Uncooperative runtime.** A runtime that ignores `reclaimTargetCount` never releases quota, so
+  the preemptor stays `Pending`. This is opt-in; only workloads whose runtime honors the field
+  should set the annotation. A future enhancement could fall back to whole eviction after a timeout.
+- **Feature isolation.** All behavior is behind the `PartialPreemption` gate; when off, the 
+  preemption path is the existing one.
+
+## Design Details
+
+### Two-phase model: request != release
+
+The signal and the quota release are intentionally decoupled to avoid any over-subscription window:
+
+- **request:** Kueue writes `reclaimTargetCount` (= `minCount`) on the victim's admission. It does
+  **not** change the accounted `count` and does **not** release quota at this moment.
+- **release:** The runtime reads `reclaimTargetCount`, releases the pod, and then resets specCount,
+  thereby release quota.
+
+```mermaid
+sequenceDiagram
+    participant P as preemptor
+    participant K as Kueue
+    participant WL as victim Workload
+    participant D as job runtime
+    P->>K: needs quota, triggers preemption
+    K->>WL: request phase writes reclaimTargetCount=minCount, count unchanged and quota not released
+    K-->>P: keep Pending
+    WL-->>D: runtime reads reclaimTargetCount
+    D->>D: shed excess replicas down to the target
+    D->>WL: release phase after Pods exit, replica count drops to actual occupancy
+    WL->>K: accounted count decreases and quota is freed
+    K->>P: admit preemptor
+    Note over K,D: occupancy never exceeds accounted count, so no over-subscription
+```
+
+### Workload API
+
+Add an optional, Kueue-owned field to `PodSetAssignment`:
+
+```go
+type PodSetAssignment struct {
+    // ...
+
+    // reclaimTargetCount, when set and lower than count, requests the elastic job to scale this
+    // PodSet down to reclaimTargetCount so partial preemption can reclaim its quota. Kueue owns
+    // this field; the job runtime reads it and sheds pods down to reclaimTargetCount. It is
+    // re-derived on every scheduling decision (equal to count when no partial preemption is
+    // requested), so it never holds a stale value.
+    // This is an alpha field and requires enabling the PartialPreemption feature gate.
+    //
+    // +optional
+    // +kubebuilder:validation:Minimum=0
+    ReclaimTargetCount *int32 `json:"reclaimTargetCount,omitempty"`
+}
+```
+
+### Opt-in annotation
+
+A Workload opts in via an annotation, e.g. `kueue.x-k8s.io/partial-preemption: "true"`. The job's
+integration layer sets it on the Job; the jobframework reconciler propagates it onto the Workload so
+that the scheduler only ever needs to look at the Workload.
+
+### Reusing minCount
+
+Partial preemption reuses the existing `PodSet.MinCount` (see
```

  `PodSet.minCount` is a kueue field for partial admission and it's not translated to the minCount of Job's pod set. 
  For example, for Ray, the minCount of WorkingGroup could be 0, however the RayCluster doesn't support partial admission functionality, it could support only Partial ScaleUp. So the Spec.ProdSet.MinCount will equal PodSet.Count.
  
  In short, we should not use PodSet.minCount value for partial preemption.

+

+

+down to 2, quota is released as those executor Pods exit, and the high-priority job is admitted. The
+Spark application keeps running the entire time.
+

+
+- The victim must opt in (annotation) and have a PodSet with `minCount` set, and must currently be
+  using more than `minCount`. Otherwise it is treated as a normal (whole-eviction) preemption target.
+- `reclaimTargetCount` is owned and re-derived by Kueue on every scheduling decision; it is not a
+  user-set field and never holds a stale value across re-admission.
+- Partial preemption interoperates with elastic-job scale-down (the accounted `count` only decreases
+  as Pods actually terminate), which is what keeps accounting correct — see below.
+- **Reclaim granularity (open question).** This proposal scales the reclaimable PodSet all the way
+  down to `minCount` in a single request, even when reclaiming fewer replicas would already free
+  enough quota for the preemptor. A progressive/incremental variant — reclaiming only as many
+  replicas as needed, with `minCount` as the floor — could reduce disruption but complicates the

+

+
+- **Over-subscription window.** Naively releasing quota when the request is written would let the
+  preemptor and the not-yet-shrunk victim overlap. Mitigated by the [two-phase
+  model](#two-phase-model-request--release): the request is decoupled from the release; accounted

+- **Uncooperative runtime.** A runtime that ignores `reclaimTargetCount` never releases quota, so
+  the preemptor stays `Pending`. This is opt-in; only workloads whose runtime honors the field
+  should set the annotation. A future enhancement could fall back to whole eviction after a timeout.
+- **Feature isolation.** All behavior is behind the `PartialPreemption` gate; when off, the 
+  preemption path is the existing one.
+
+## Design Details
+
+### Two-phase model: request != release
+

+

+  thereby release quota.
+
+```mermaid
+sequenceDiagram
+    participant P as preemptor
+    participant K as Kueue
+    participant WL as victim Workload
+    participant D as job runtime
+    P->>K: needs quota, triggers preemption
+    K->>WL: request phase writes reclaimTargetCount=minCount, count unchanged and quota not released
+    K-->>P: keep Pending
+    WL-->>D: runtime reads reclaimTargetCount
+    D->>D: shed excess replicas down to the target
+    D->>WL: release phase after Pods exit, replica count drops to actual occupancy
+    WL->>K: accounted count decreases and quota is freed

+```
+
+### Workload API
+
+Add an optional, Kueue-owned field to `PodSetAssignment`:
+
+```go
+type PodSetAssignment struct {
+    // ...
+
+    // reclaimTargetCount, when set and lower than count, requests the elastic job to scale this
+    // PodSet down to reclaimTargetCount so partial preemption can reclaim its quota. Kueue owns
+    // this field; the job runtime reads it and sheds pods down to reclaimTargetCount. It is
+    // re-derived on every scheduling decision (equal to count when no partial preemption is
+    // requested), so it never holds a stale value.
+    // This is an alpha field and requires enabling the PartialPreemption feature gate.
+    //
+    // +optional
+    // +kubebuilder:validation:Minimum=0
+    ReclaimTargetCount *int32 `json:"reclaimTargetCount,omitempty"`
+}
+```
+
+### Opt-in annotation
+
+A Workload opts in via an annotation, e.g. `kueue.x-k8s.io/partial-preemption: "true"`. The job's
+integration layer sets it on the Job; the jobframework reconciler propagates it onto the Workload so
+that the scheduler only ever needs to look at the Workload.
+
+### Reusing minCount
+
+Partial preemption reuses the existing `PodSet.MinCount` (see
+[KEP-420](/keps/420-partial-admission)) as the scale-down floor. `minCount` is preserved when either
+`PartialAdmission` or `PartialPreemption` is enabled. As with partial admission, at most one PodSet
+per Workload may set `minCount`, so partial preemption reclaims a single PodSet per Workload.
+
+### Scheduler / Preemption
+
+#### Eligibility
+
+A candidate is eligible for partial preemption when: the `PartialPreemption` gate is enabled, the
+Workload carries the opt-in annotation, its reclaimable PodSet has `minCount` set, and its currently
+used count is above `minCount`. "Used" is `min(admittedCount, specCount - reclaimablePods)`,The
+amount reclaimable is `used - minCount`, and the requested `reclaimTargetCount` is `minCount`.
+
+#### Candidate ordering
+
+Within the existing preemption candidate ordering, a criterion is inserted **after priority** and
+before the "more recently admitted first": among equal-priority candidates, a partial-preemptible 
+one is preferred (partial preemption is cheaper than a full eviction). Placing it after priority

@@ -0,0 +1,306 @@
+# KEP-975: Partial Preemption of Elastic Workloads
+
+<!-- toc -->
+- [Summary](#summary)
+- [Motivation](#motivation)
+  - [Goals](#goals)
+  - [Non-Goals](#non-goals)
+- [Proposal](#proposal)
+  - [User Stories](#user-stories)
+    - [Story 1: Spark on Kubernetes elastic executors](#story-1-spark-on-kubernetes-elastic-executors)
+  - [Notes/Constraints/Caveats](#notesconstraintscaveats)
+  - [Risks and Mitigations](#risks-and-mitigations)
+- [Design Details](#design-details)
+  - [Two-phase model: request != release](#two-phase-model-request--release)
+  - [Workload API](#workload-api)
+  - [Opt-in annotation](#opt-in-annotation)
+  - [Reusing minCount](#reusing-mincount)
+  - [Scheduler / Preemption](#scheduler--preemption)
+    - [Eligibility](#eligibility)
+    - [Candidate ordering](#candidate-ordering)
+    - [Target selection](#target-selection)
+    - [Issuing preemptions](#issuing-preemptions)
+    - [No-fit progress across scheduling cycles](#no-fit-progress-across-scheduling-cycles)
+  - [Webhook](#webhook)
+  - [Job runtime contract](#job-runtime-contract)
+  - [Test Plan](#test-plan)
+    - [Unit tests](#unit-tests)
+    - [Integration tests](#integration-tests)
+    - [e2e tests](#e2e-tests)
+  - [Graduation Criteria](#graduation-criteria)
+- [Implementation History](#implementation-history)
+- [Drawbacks](#drawbacks)
+- [Alternatives](#alternatives)
+  - [Kueue mutates the job's replica count directly](#kueue-mutates-the-jobs-replica-count-directly)
+  - [Incremental reclamation instead of scaling to minCount](#incremental-reclamation-instead-of-scaling-to-mincount)
+<!-- /toc -->
+
+## Summary
+
+Today Kueue preempts a Workload as a whole: the victim is evicted, `stopJob` suspends it, and all
+of its Pods are torn down. For elastic workloads that can run at a reduced size (for example a Spark
+application whose executor count can shrink), whole eviction is unnecessarily disruptive.
+
+**Partial Preemption** lets Kueue reclaim quota from an elastic, opted-in Workload by asking it to
+scale one PodSet **down to its `minCount`** — freeing just enough quota for the preemptor — instead
+of evicting it. The victim keeps running at a smaller size and is never suspended. The feature is
+alpha and gated by the `PartialPreemption` feature gate; when the gate is off, behavior is identical
+to today.
+
+## Motivation
+
+Elastic batch/data workloads (Spark, Ray, and similar) can often continue making progress
+with fewer replicas. When a higher-priority Workload needs quota, Kueue's only tool is to evict a
+victim entirely. For a long-running elastic job this means killing the driver and every worker,
+losing in-flight progress, and paying a full restart — even though releasing a few replicas would
+have been enough.
+
+Kueue cannot shrink a running job on its own: only the job's controller/driver owns its Pods.
+Partial preemption therefore needs a way for Kueue to **signal** a desired reduced size and for the
+job runtime to **act** on it, while the quota accounting stays correct throughout.
+
+### Goals
+
+- Provide an opt-in mechanism where preemption of an elastic Workload reclaims quota by scaling one
+  of its PodSets down to `minCount`, rather than evicting the whole Workload.
+- Guarantee no quota over-subscription at any point during the scale-down.
+- Keep the mechanism framework-agnostic in Kueue: Kueue only expresses intent; any controller/driver
+  that honors the signal can benefit.
+- Be fully gated so that, with the gate disabled, there is zero behavioral change.
+
+### Non-Goals
+
+- Kueue does not itself terminate Pods or mutate the parent job's spec; the job runtime is
+  responsible for shedding replicas (see [Alternatives](#alternatives) for why).
+- Kueue does not guarantee the job respects the requested reduced size; a cooperating runtime is
+  assumed (opt-in).
+- Autoscaling / scaling back up after preemption is out of scope; this KEP only covers scale-down
+  for quota reclamation.
+
+## Proposal
+
+Introduce a per-PodSet target, `status.admission.podSetAssignments[].reclaimTargetCount`, that Kueue
+writes on an admitted elastic Workload to request that the PodSet scale down to that count
+(= `minCount`). The job runtime observes `reclaimTargetCount`, sheds the excess replicas, and as
+those Pods actually terminate the accounted `count` decreases and quota is released — at which point
+the preemptor is admitted.
+
+The preemption decision path (both the classical and the Fair Sharing algorithms) is extended so
+that an eligible elastic victim is simulated as *reduced to `minCount`* in the scheduling snapshot
+instead of *removed*, and such victims are preferred over whole eviction of equal-priority peers.
+
+### User Stories
+
+#### Story 1: Spark on Kubernetes elastic executors
+
+A Spark application runs with 20 executors (`minExecutors=2`) in a low-priority ClusterQueue that is
+borrowing quota. A high-priority job arrives and needs part of that quota. Instead of suspending the
+whole Spark application (killing the driver and all executors and losing progress), Kueue writes
+`reclaimTargetCount=2` on the executor PodSet. The Spark driver gracefully decommissions executors
+down to 2, quota is released as those executor Pods exit, and the high-priority job is admitted. The
+Spark application keeps running the entire time.
+
+### Notes/Constraints/Caveats
+
+- The victim must opt in (annotation) and have a PodSet with `minCount` set, and must currently be
+  using more than `minCount`. Otherwise it is treated as a normal (whole-eviction) preemption target.
+- `reclaimTargetCount` is owned and re-derived by Kueue on every scheduling decision; it is not a
+  user-set field and never holds a stale value across re-admission.
+- Partial preemption interoperates with elastic-job scale-down (the accounted `count` only decreases
+  as Pods actually terminate), which is what keeps accounting correct — see below.
+- **Reclaim granularity (open question).** This proposal scales the reclaimable PodSet all the way
+  down to `minCount` in a single request, even when reclaiming fewer replicas would already free
+  enough quota for the preemptor. A progressive/incremental variant — reclaiming only as many
+  replicas as needed, with `minCount` as the floor — could reduce disruption but complicates the
+  accumulate-and-fit search and the convergence argument. This is left as an open question for the
+  alpha review.
+
+### Risks and Mitigations
+
+- **Over-subscription window.** Naively releasing quota when the request is written would let the
+  preemptor and the not-yet-shrunk victim overlap. Mitigated by the [two-phase
+  model](#two-phase-model-request--release): the request is decoupled from the release; accounted
+  `count` only drops as Pods exit.
+- **Uncooperative runtime.** A runtime that ignores `reclaimTargetCount` never releases quota, so
+  the preemptor stays `Pending`. This is opt-in; only workloads whose runtime honors the field
+  should set the annotation. A future enhancement could fall back to whole eviction after a timeout.
+- **Feature isolation.** All behavior is behind the `PartialPreemption` gate; when off, the 
+  preemption path is the existing one.
+
+## Design Details
+
+### Two-phase model: request != release
+
+The signal and the quota release are intentionally decoupled to avoid any over-subscription window:
+
+- **request:** Kueue writes `reclaimTargetCount` (= `minCount`) on the victim's admission. It does
+  **not** change the accounted `count` and does **not** release quota at this moment.
+- **release:** The runtime reads `reclaimTargetCount`, releases the pod, and then resets specCount,
+  thereby release quota.
+
+```mermaid
+sequenceDiagram
+    participant P as preemptor
+    participant K as Kueue
+    participant WL as victim Workload
+    participant D as job runtime
+    P->>K: needs quota, triggers preemption
+    K->>WL: request phase writes reclaimTargetCount=minCount, count unchanged and quota not released
+    K-->>P: keep Pending
+    WL-->>D: runtime reads reclaimTargetCount
+    D->>D: shed excess replicas down to the target
+    D->>WL: release phase after Pods exit, replica count drops to actual occupancy
+    WL->>K: accounted count decreases and quota is freed
+    K->>P: admit preemptor
+    Note over K,D: occupancy never exceeds accounted count, so no over-subscription
+```
+
+### Workload API
+
+Add an optional, Kueue-owned field to `PodSetAssignment`:
+
+```go
+type PodSetAssignment struct {
+    // ...
+
+    // reclaimTargetCount, when set and lower than count, requests the elastic job to scale this
+    // PodSet down to reclaimTargetCount so partial preemption can reclaim its quota. Kueue owns
+    // this field; the job runtime reads it and sheds pods down to reclaimTargetCount. It is
+    // re-derived on every scheduling decision (equal to count when no partial preemption is
+    // requested), so it never holds a stale value.
+    // This is an alpha field and requires enabling the PartialPreemption feature gate.
+    //
+    // +optional
+    // +kubebuilder:validation:Minimum=0
+    ReclaimTargetCount *int32 `json:"reclaimTargetCount,omitempty"`
+}
+```
+
+### Opt-in annotation
+
+A Workload opts in via an annotation, e.g. `kueue.x-k8s.io/partial-preemption: "true"`. The job's
+integration layer sets it on the Job; the jobframework reconciler propagates it onto the Workload so
+that the scheduler only ever needs to look at the Workload.
+
+### Reusing minCount
+
+Partial preemption reuses the existing `PodSet.MinCount` (see
+[KEP-420](/keps/420-partial-admission)) as the scale-down floor. `minCount` is preserved when either
+`PartialAdmission` or `PartialPreemption` is enabled. As with partial admission, at most one PodSet
+per Workload may set `minCount`, so partial preemption reclaims a single PodSet per Workload.
+
+### Scheduler / Preemption
+
+#### Eligibility
+
+A candidate is eligible for partial preemption when: the `PartialPreemption` gate is enabled, the
+Workload carries the opt-in annotation, its reclaimable PodSet has `minCount` set, and its currently
+used count is above `minCount`. "Used" is `min(admittedCount, specCount - reclaimablePods)`,The
+amount reclaimable is `used - minCount`, and the requested `reclaimTargetCount` is `minCount`.
+
+#### Candidate ordering
+
+Within the existing preemption candidate ordering, a criterion is inserted **after priority** and
+before the "more recently admitted first": among equal-priority candidates, a partial-preemptible 
+one is preferred (partial preemption is cheaper than a full eviction). Placing it after priority
+ensures priority still dominates. With the gate off this criterion is a no-op.
+
+#### Target selection
+
+Both preemption algorithms funnel "remove a candidate" through a single primitive. For an eligible
+candidate, the scheduler simulates the *reduced* Workload (scaled to `minCount`) in the snapshot
+instead of removing it; otherwise it removes the whole Workload as today. Reductions accumulate
+across candidates before the fit check. A partial-preemptible candidate is only ever whole-evicted
+in a later cycle, once it has already been scaled to `minCount` and is no longer
+partial-preemptible.
+
+#### Issuing preemptions
+
+For a partial target, Kueue does **not** evict or `stopJob`. It patches the victim's admission
+status to set `reclaimTargetCount` and emits a `PartiallyPreempted` event. Quota is not released
+here (that is the release phase of the two-phase model).
+
+#### No-fit progress across scheduling cycles
+
+When an algorithm cannot fully fit the preemptor in a cycle but has accumulated partial targets, it
```

  This part is unclear for me. Do you mean that preemptor won't fit anyway, but the kueue will issue partial preemption?

+
+Elastic batch/data workloads (Spark, Ray, and similar) can often continue making progress
+with fewer replicas. When a higher-priority Workload needs quota, Kueue's only tool is to evict a
+victim entirely. For a long-running elastic job this means killing the driver and every worker,
+losing in-flight progress, and paying a full restart — even though releasing a few replicas would
+have been enough.
+
+Kueue cannot shrink a running job on its own: only the job's controller/driver owns its Pods.
+Partial preemption therefore needs a way for Kueue to **signal** a desired reduced size and for the
+job runtime to **act** on it, while the quota accounting stays correct throughout.
+
+### Goals
+
+- Provide an opt-in mechanism where preemption of an elastic Workload reclaims quota by scaling one
+  of its PodSets down to `minCount`, rather than evicting the whole Workload.
+- Guarantee no quota over-subscription at any point during the scale-down.
+- Keep the mechanism framework-agnostic in Kueue: Kueue only expresses intent; any controller/driver
+  that honors the signal can benefit.
+- Be fully gated so that, with the gate disabled, there is zero behavioral change.
+
+### Non-Goals
+
+- Kueue does not itself terminate Pods or mutate the parent job's spec; the job runtime is
+  responsible for shedding replicas (see [Alternatives](#alternatives) for why).
+- Kueue does not guarantee the job respects the requested reduced size; a cooperating runtime is
+  assumed (opt-in).
+- Autoscaling / scaling back up after preemption is out of scope; this KEP only covers scale-down
+  for quota reclamation.
+
+## Proposal
+
+Introduce a per-PodSet target, `status.admission.podSetAssignments[].reclaimTargetCount`, that Kueue
+writes on an admitted elastic Workload to request that the PodSet scale down to that count
+(= `minCount`). The job runtime observes `reclaimTargetCount`, sheds the excess replicas, and as
+those Pods actually terminate the accounted `count` decreases and quota is released — at which point
+the preemptor is admitted.
+
+The preemption decision path (both the classical and the Fair Sharing algorithms) is extended so
+that an eligible elastic victim is simulated as *reduced to `minCount`* in the scheduling snapshot
+instead of *removed*, and such victims are preferred over whole eviction of equal-priority peers.
+
+### User Stories
+
+#### Story 1: Spark on Kubernetes elastic executors
+
+A Spark application runs with 20 executors (`minExecutors=2`) in a low-priority ClusterQueue that is
+borrowing quota. A high-priority job arrives and needs part of that quota. Instead of suspending the
+whole Spark application (killing the driver and all executors and losing progress), Kueue writes
+`reclaimTargetCount=2` on the executor PodSet. The Spark driver gracefully decommissions executors
+down to 2, quota is released as those executor Pods exit, and the high-priority job is admitted. The
+Spark application keeps running the entire time.
+
+### Notes/Constraints/Caveats
+
+- The victim must opt in (annotation) and have a PodSet with `minCount` set, and must currently be
+  using more than `minCount`. Otherwise it is treated as a normal (whole-eviction) preemption target.
+- `reclaimTargetCount` is owned and re-derived by Kueue on every scheduling decision; it is not a
+  user-set field and never holds a stale value across re-admission.
+- Partial preemption interoperates with elastic-job scale-down (the accounted `count` only decreases
+  as Pods actually terminate), which is what keeps accounting correct — see below.
+- **Reclaim granularity (open question).** This proposal scales the reclaimable PodSet all the way
+  down to `minCount` in a single request, even when reclaiming fewer replicas would already free
+  enough quota for the preemptor. A progressive/incremental variant — reclaiming only as many
+  replicas as needed, with `minCount` as the floor — could reduce disruption but complicates the
+  accumulate-and-fit search and the convergence argument. This is left as an open question for the
+  alpha review.
+
+### Risks and Mitigations
+
+- **Over-subscription window.** Naively releasing quota when the request is written would let the
+  preemptor and the not-yet-shrunk victim overlap. Mitigated by the [two-phase
+  model](#two-phase-model-request--release): the request is decoupled from the release; accounted
+  `count` only drops as Pods exit.
+- **Uncooperative runtime.** A runtime that ignores `reclaimTargetCount` never releases quota, so
+  the preemptor stays `Pending`. This is opt-in; only workloads whose runtime honors the field
+  should set the annotation. A future enhancement could fall back to whole eviction after a timeout.
+- **Feature isolation.** All behavior is behind the `PartialPreemption` gate; when off, the 
+  preemption path is the existing one.
+
+## Design Details
+
+### Two-phase model: request != release
+
+The signal and the quota release are intentionally decoupled to avoid any over-subscription window:
+
+- **request:** Kueue writes `reclaimTargetCount` (= `minCount`) on the victim's admission. It does
+  **not** change the accounted `count` and does **not** release quota at this moment.
+- **release:** The runtime reads `reclaimTargetCount`, releases the pod, and then resets specCount,
+  thereby release quota.
+
+```mermaid
+sequenceDiagram
+    participant P as preemptor
+    participant K as Kueue
+    participant WL as victim Workload
+    participant D as job runtime
+    P->>K: needs quota, triggers preemption
+    K->>WL: request phase writes reclaimTargetCount=minCount, count unchanged and quota not released
+    K-->>P: keep Pending
+    WL-->>D: runtime reads reclaimTargetCount
+    D->>D: shed excess replicas down to the target
+    D->>WL: release phase after Pods exit, replica count drops to actual occupancy
+    WL->>K: accounted count decreases and quota is freed
+    K->>P: admit preemptor
+    Note over K,D: occupancy never exceeds accounted count, so no over-subscription
+```
+
+### Workload API
+
+Add an optional, Kueue-owned field to `PodSetAssignment`:
+
+```go
+type PodSetAssignment struct {
+    // ...
+
+    // reclaimTargetCount, when set and lower than count, requests the elastic job to scale this
+    // PodSet down to reclaimTargetCount so partial preemption can reclaim its quota. Kueue owns
+    // this field; the job runtime reads it and sheds pods down to reclaimTargetCount. It is
+    // re-derived on every scheduling decision (equal to count when no partial preemption is
+    // requested), so it never holds a stale value.
+    // This is an alpha field and requires enabling the PartialPreemption feature gate.
+    //
+    // +optional
+    // +kubebuilder:validation:Minimum=0
+    ReclaimTargetCount *int32 `json:"reclaimTargetCount,omitempty"`
+}
+```
+
+### Opt-in annotation
+
+A Workload opts in via an annotation, e.g. `kueue.x-k8s.io/partial-preemption: "true"`. The job's
+integration layer sets it on the Job; the jobframework reconciler propagates it onto the Workload so
+that the scheduler only ever needs to look at the Workload.
+
+### Reusing minCount
+
+Partial preemption reuses the existing `PodSet.MinCount` (see
```

  MinCount represents the minimum number of runnable instances; I believe it is reasonable to allow partial preemption to reduce the count down to this minimum. If partial admission functionality is not supported, minCount does not need to be set.

+
+## Summary
+
+Today Kueue preempts a Workload as a whole: the victim is evicted, `stopJob` suspends it, and all
+of its Pods are torn down. For elastic workloads that can run at a reduced size (for example a Spark
+application whose executor count can shrink), whole eviction is unnecessarily disruptive.
+
+**Partial Preemption** lets Kueue reclaim quota from an elastic, opted-in Workload by asking it to
+scale one PodSet **down to its `minCount`** — freeing just enough quota for the preemptor — instead
+of evicting it. The victim keeps running at a smaller size and is never suspended. The feature is
+alpha and gated by the `PartialPreemption` feature gate; when the gate is off, behavior is identical
+to today.
+
+## Motivation
+
+Elastic batch/data workloads (Spark, Ray, and similar) can often continue making progress
+with fewer replicas. When a higher-priority Workload needs quota, Kueue's only tool is to evict a
+victim entirely. For a long-running elastic job this means killing the driver and every worker,
+losing in-flight progress, and paying a full restart — even though releasing a few replicas would
+have been enough.
+
+Kueue cannot shrink a running job on its own: only the job's controller/driver owns its Pods.
+Partial preemption therefore needs a way for Kueue to **signal** a desired reduced size and for the
+job runtime to **act** on it, while the quota accounting stays correct throughout.
+
+### Goals
+
+- Provide an opt-in mechanism where preemption of an elastic Workload reclaims quota by scaling one
+  of its PodSets down to `minCount`, rather than evicting the whole Workload.
+- Guarantee no quota over-subscription at any point during the scale-down.
+- Keep the mechanism framework-agnostic in Kueue: Kueue only expresses intent; any controller/driver
+  that honors the signal can benefit.
+- Be fully gated so that, with the gate disabled, there is zero behavioral change.
+
+### Non-Goals
+
+- Kueue does not itself terminate Pods or mutate the parent job's spec; the job runtime is
+  responsible for shedding replicas (see [Alternatives](#alternatives) for why).
+- Kueue does not guarantee the job respects the requested reduced size; a cooperating runtime is
+  assumed (opt-in).
+- Autoscaling / scaling back up after preemption is out of scope; this KEP only covers scale-down
+  for quota reclamation.
+
+## Proposal
+
+Introduce a per-PodSet target, `status.admission.podSetAssignments[].reclaimTargetCount`, that Kueue
+writes on an admitted elastic Workload to request that the PodSet scale down to that count
+(= `minCount`). The job runtime observes `reclaimTargetCount`, sheds the excess replicas, and as
+those Pods actually terminate the accounted `count` decreases and quota is released — at which point
+the preemptor is admitted.
+
+The preemption decision path (both the classical and the Fair Sharing algorithms) is extended so
+that an eligible elastic victim is simulated as *reduced to `minCount`* in the scheduling snapshot
+instead of *removed*, and such victims are preferred over whole eviction of equal-priority peers.
+
+### User Stories
+
+#### Story 1: Spark on Kubernetes elastic executors
+
+A Spark application runs with 20 executors (`minExecutors=2`) in a low-priority ClusterQueue that is
+borrowing quota. A high-priority job arrives and needs part of that quota. Instead of suspending the
+whole Spark application (killing the driver and all executors and losing progress), Kueue writes
+`reclaimTargetCount=2` on the executor PodSet. The Spark driver gracefully decommissions executors
+down to 2, quota is released as those executor Pods exit, and the high-priority job is admitted. The
+Spark application keeps running the entire time.
+
+### Notes/Constraints/Caveats
+
+- The victim must opt in (annotation) and have a PodSet with `minCount` set, and must currently be
+  using more than `minCount`. Otherwise it is treated as a normal (whole-eviction) preemption target.
+- `reclaimTargetCount` is owned and re-derived by Kueue on every scheduling decision; it is not a
+  user-set field and never holds a stale value across re-admission.
+- Partial preemption interoperates with elastic-job scale-down (the accounted `count` only decreases
+  as Pods actually terminate), which is what keeps accounting correct — see below.
+- **Reclaim granularity (open question).** This proposal scales the reclaimable PodSet all the way
+  down to `minCount` in a single request, even when reclaiming fewer replicas would already free
+  enough quota for the preemptor. A progressive/incremental variant — reclaiming only as many
+  replicas as needed, with `minCount` as the floor — could reduce disruption but complicates the
+  accumulate-and-fit search and the convergence argument. This is left as an open question for the
+  alpha review.
+
+### Risks and Mitigations
+
+- **Over-subscription window.** Naively releasing quota when the request is written would let the
+  preemptor and the not-yet-shrunk victim overlap. Mitigated by the [two-phase
+  model](#two-phase-model-request--release): the request is decoupled from the release; accounted
+  `count` only drops as Pods exit.
+- **Uncooperative runtime.** A runtime that ignores `reclaimTargetCount` never releases quota, so
+  the preemptor stays `Pending`. This is opt-in; only workloads whose runtime honors the field
+  should set the annotation. A future enhancement could fall back to whole eviction after a timeout.
+- **Feature isolation.** All behavior is behind the `PartialPreemption` gate; when off, the 
+  preemption path is the existing one.
+
+## Design Details
+
+### Two-phase model: request != release
+
+The signal and the quota release are intentionally decoupled to avoid any over-subscription window:
+
+- **request:** Kueue writes `reclaimTargetCount` (= `minCount`) on the victim's admission. It does
+  **not** change the accounted `count` and does **not** release quota at this moment.
+- **release:** The runtime reads `reclaimTargetCount`, releases the pod, and then resets specCount,
+  thereby release quota.
+
+```mermaid
+sequenceDiagram
+    participant P as preemptor
+    participant K as Kueue
+    participant WL as victim Workload
+    participant D as job runtime
+    P->>K: needs quota, triggers preemption
+    K->>WL: request phase writes reclaimTargetCount=minCount, count unchanged and quota not released
+    K-->>P: keep Pending
+    WL-->>D: runtime reads reclaimTargetCount
+    D->>D: shed excess replicas down to the target
+    D->>WL: release phase after Pods exit, replica count drops to actual occupancy
+    WL->>K: accounted count decreases and quota is freed
+    K->>P: admit preemptor
+    Note over K,D: occupancy never exceeds accounted count, so no over-subscription
+```
+
+### Workload API
+
+Add an optional, Kueue-owned field to `PodSetAssignment`:
+
+```go
+type PodSetAssignment struct {
+    // ...
+
+    // reclaimTargetCount, when set and lower than count, requests the elastic job to scale this
+    // PodSet down to reclaimTargetCount so partial preemption can reclaim its quota. Kueue owns
+    // this field; the job runtime reads it and sheds pods down to reclaimTargetCount. It is
+    // re-derived on every scheduling decision (equal to count when no partial preemption is
+    // requested), so it never holds a stale value.
+    // This is an alpha field and requires enabling the PartialPreemption feature gate.
+    //
+    // +optional
+    // +kubebuilder:validation:Minimum=0
+    ReclaimTargetCount *int32 `json:"reclaimTargetCount,omitempty"`
+}
+```
+
+### Opt-in annotation
+
+A Workload opts in via an annotation, e.g. `kueue.x-k8s.io/partial-preemption: "true"`. The job's
+integration layer sets it on the Job; the jobframework reconciler propagates it onto the Workload so
+that the scheduler only ever needs to look at the Workload.
+
+### Reusing minCount
+
+Partial preemption reuses the existing `PodSet.MinCount` (see
+[KEP-420](/keps/420-partial-admission)) as the scale-down floor. `minCount` is preserved when either
+`PartialAdmission` or `PartialPreemption` is enabled. As with partial admission, at most one PodSet
+per Workload may set `minCount`, so partial preemption reclaims a single PodSet per Workload.
+
+### Scheduler / Preemption
+
+#### Eligibility
+
+A candidate is eligible for partial preemption when: the `PartialPreemption` gate is enabled, the
+Workload carries the opt-in annotation, its reclaimable PodSet has `minCount` set, and its currently
+used count is above `minCount`. "Used" is `min(admittedCount, specCount - reclaimablePods)`,The
+amount reclaimable is `used - minCount`, and the requested `reclaimTargetCount` is `minCount`.
+
+#### Candidate ordering
+
+Within the existing preemption candidate ordering, a criterion is inserted **after priority** and
+before the "more recently admitted first": among equal-priority candidates, a partial-preemptible 
+one is preferred (partial preemption is cheaper than a full eviction). Placing it after priority
+ensures priority still dominates. With the gate off this criterion is a no-op.
+
+#### Target selection
```

  It occurs within `getTargets`, prior to the "put back" step.

+    // requested), so it never holds a stale value.
+    // This is an alpha field and requires enabling the PartialPreemption feature gate.
+    //
+    // +optional
+    // +kubebuilder:validation:Minimum=0
+    ReclaimTargetCount *int32 `json:"reclaimTargetCount,omitempty"`
+}
+```
+
+### Opt-in annotation
+
+A Workload opts in via an annotation, e.g. `kueue.x-k8s.io/partial-preemption: "true"`. The job's
+integration layer sets it on the Job; the jobframework reconciler propagates it onto the Workload so
+that the scheduler only ever needs to look at the Workload.
+
+### Reusing minCount
+
+Partial preemption reuses the existing `PodSet.MinCount` (see
+[KEP-420](/keps/420-partial-admission)) as the scale-down floor. `minCount` is preserved when either
+`PartialAdmission` or `PartialPreemption` is enabled. As with partial admission, at most one PodSet
+per Workload may set `minCount`, so partial preemption reclaims a single PodSet per Workload.
+
+### Scheduler / Preemption
+
+#### Eligibility
+
+A candidate is eligible for partial preemption when: the `PartialPreemption` gate is enabled, the
+Workload carries the opt-in annotation, its reclaimable PodSet has `minCount` set, and its currently
+used count is above `minCount`. "Used" is `min(admittedCount, specCount - reclaimablePods)`,The
+amount reclaimable is `used - minCount`, and the requested `reclaimTargetCount` is `minCount`.
+
+#### Candidate ordering
+
+Within the existing preemption candidate ordering, a criterion is inserted **after priority** and
+before the "more recently admitted first": among equal-priority candidates, a partial-preemptible 
+one is preferred (partial preemption is cheaper than a full eviction). Placing it after priority
+ensures priority still dominates. With the gate off this criterion is a no-op.
+
+#### Target selection
+
+Both preemption algorithms funnel "remove a candidate" through a single primitive. For an eligible
+candidate, the scheduler simulates the *reduced* Workload (scaled to `minCount`) in the snapshot
+instead of removing it; otherwise it removes the whole Workload as today. Reductions accumulate
+across candidates before the fit check. A partial-preemptible candidate is only ever whole-evicted
+in a later cycle, once it has already been scaled to `minCount` and is no longer
+partial-preemptible.
+
+#### Issuing preemptions
+
+For a partial target, Kueue does **not** evict or `stopJob`. It patches the victim's admission
+status to set `reclaimTargetCount` and emits a `PartiallyPreempted` event. Quota is not released
+here (that is the release phase of the two-phase model).

+
